One of the cheapest ways to fly Star Alliance business and first class

Picture this: you settle into a Lufthansa Boeing 747-8 business class seat on the upper deck, somewhere over the Atlantic, a glass of Champagne in hand, your fully flat bed already made up behind you. New York to Frankfurt — a route that commands $4,000 to $8,000 in cash for a business class ticket — and you're here for 63,000 Avianca LifeMiles. No fuel surcharges. No carrier-imposed fees eating into your redemption. Just the experience. That's the moment the FI community lives for. Not the luxury itself — though Lufthansa's business class product is genuinely exceptional — but the *math*. The realization that the loyalty system has a pricing gap so wide you could fly a 747 through it. Here's what the numbers actually mean. At the low end, a $4,000 business class ticket redeemed for 63,000 points works out to **6.3 cents per point (CPP)**. At the high end — peak travel season, last-minute pricing, transatlantic demand — that same seat hits $8,000 in cash, which means your LifeMiles are delivering **12.7 cents per point**. For context, most travel rewards experts consider 1–2 CPP a baseline for points value. You're achieving 6 to 12 times that baseline. The reason this feels like a genuine inefficiency rather than just a deal: Avianca LifeMiles prices Star Alliance awards on a **fixed award chart** that doesn't fluctuate with cash fare demand. When airlines raise transatlantic business class fares during summer holidays or major events, your points cost stays exactly the same. The gap between what the market charges and what LifeMiles charges simply grows wider. And then there's the no-fuel-surcharge policy — the detail that separates LifeMiles from competitors like British Airways Avios, which can pile $600+ in carrier surcharges onto a single redemption. With LifeMiles, you pay government taxes only, typically $50–$150 for a transatlantic itinerary. That's it. For the ChooseFI community, this redemption represents exactly the kind of asymmetric value that underpins a great travel rewards strategy: use points where cash prices are highest, eliminate fees wherever possible, and fly a product you'd never justify paying full price for — because you didn't have to.

The core insight here is understanding why Avianca LifeMiles prices Star Alliance awards so aggressively — and then understanding how to get points into the program quickly enough to actually use them.

Avianca is a Star Alliance member, which means it has reciprocal award booking agreements with every other Star Alliance carrier — United, Lufthansa, Swiss, ANA, EVA Air, Singapore Airlines, and more. Like all alliance programs, LifeMiles can sell award seats on partner airlines. The pricing is set by Avianca's own award chart, not by Lufthansa's chart or United's chart.

This is the pricing inefficiency. Lufthansa's own frequent flyer program (Miles & More) charges over 100,000 miles for the same transatlantic business class route and layers on fuel surcharges. United MileagePlus charges 70,000–88,000 miles for the same seat. Avianca LifeMiles charges 63,000 miles with zero carrier surcharges. Same seat. Same plane. Wildly different cost.

Avianca keeps rates competitive because they want their program to be attractive to non-Avianca flyers — it's a revenue play. And because their route network is South America-focused, they've built a powerful award program that earns loyalty from customers who may never set foot on an Avianca plane.

Many programs claim no surcharges, then quietly pass through "carrier-imposed fees" that look indistinguishable from surcharges. LifeMiles is one of the genuine exceptions. On partner award bookings — including Lufthansa, Swiss, and other carriers that traditionally levy heavy surcharges — LifeMiles charges only government taxes. For a JFK–FRA business class award, expect to pay roughly $50–$150 out of pocket. That's the full cash outlay.

This is where the transfer partner ecosystem matters. LifeMiles accepts transfers from:

The instant Citi transfer is particularly powerful: if you find available award space today, you can move points and call LifeMiles to book within the same session. Capital One and Bilt are nearly as fast. Marriott's 3:1 ratio makes it less efficient unless you have a large Bonvoy balance and no better option.

LifeMiles runs frequent purchased miles sales, often 100%+ bonus miles on purchases. This is a legitimate strategy for topping off your balance when you're a few thousand miles short of a redemption. Combined with transfer partners, it creates significant flexibility. Watch for promotions at lifemiles.com — they typically appear 6–8 times per year.

The golden rule of any points transfer: never transfer miles until you've confirmed award availability. LifeMiles miles are non-refundable once transferred, and award space can disappear in hours.

United MileagePlus has one of the best Star Alliance partner award search tools available to the public. Go to united.com, start a search as if you're booking an award ticket, and look for business class availability on Lufthansa or Swiss flights on your target route. United displays Lufthansa partner space accurately in most cases. You're looking for "saver" level availability — marked as "X" class on Lufthansa.

Lufthansa's own website (lufthansa.com) sometimes shows availability that United misses, and vice versa. Search both. You're not booking here — just confirming the seat exists.

An ExpertFlyer subscription ($10/month) lets you set availability alerts for specific routes and dates. For a high-value redemption like this, it's often worth one month of the service to monitor your target window.

LifeMiles opens its booking window 355 days in advance. For peak travel periods — summer transatlantic, major European events — set a calendar reminder to search at the 355-day mark. Business class award space on Lufthansa releases in small batches and fills quickly.

Once you've confirmed space, act fast. Log into your points program and initiate the transfer:

Transfer exactly what you need — 63,000 miles for a one-way business class transatlantic award — plus a small buffer of 2,000–3,000 miles for potential routing variations.

Go to lifemiles.com and search your award. The website has improved but can still be inconsistent with partner availability display. If the online search doesn't show the space you found on United.com, call LifeMiles directly at their customer service line. Have your flight numbers, dates, and cabin class ready. Phone agents can access and book partner availability that the website sometimes fails to display.

After booking, you'll receive a confirmation email with a PNR (record locator). Verify this on the operating carrier's website — go to lufthansa.com or swiss.com and look up your reservation using the PNR. If it pulls up correctly, your booking is solid. Save this confirmation and check it once more 72 hours before departure.

Avianca LifeMiles uses a distance-based award chart for Star Alliance partner flights, and their chart is consistently 15-40% cheaper than competing programs for the same seats. The reason is strategic: Avianca wants to attract members to its loyalty program, and aggressive pricing on aspirational redemptions is how they compete with larger alliances. For you, this means the exact same Lufthansa First Class seat or ANA business class seat costs significantly fewer points when booked through LifeMiles versus through United MileagePlus or the carrier directly. The catch is that LifeMiles availability depends on the operating carrier releasing award space to partners, so you still need to search on the carrier or United.com before transferring.

Card links may earn ChooseFI a commission. See our advertiser disclosure.

Let's walk through a real redemption so you can see exactly what this looks like in practice.

The Route: New York JFK → Frankfurt FRA, nonstop The Aircraft: Lufthansa Boeing 747-8, upper deck business class The Cash Price: $6,500 (a realistic peak-season transatlantic business class fare) The Points Cost: 63,000 Avianca LifeMiles Out-of-Pocket Fees: ~$85 in government taxes CPP Achieved: 10.3 cents per point

You've transferred 63,000 Citi ThankYou points to LifeMiles — instantly — spotted the award space on United.com the morning it opened, called LifeMiles to confirm, and had your booking locked in within 90 minutes of finding the seat.

Lufthansa's 747-8 upper deck business class is one of the most iconic products in commercial aviation. The upper deck seats — in a 2-2-2 configuration — sit quieter and smoother than the main deck, with fewer passengers and a more intimate cabin feel.

Your seat converts to a fully flat bed of approximately 6 feet. You'll have direct aisle access (depending on your seat selection), a large personal entertainment screen, and Lufthansa's catering, which consistently ranks among the top transatlantic carriers. The meal service on JFK–FRA is legitimate: a proper multi-course dinner, a breakfast service as you approach Germany, and a snack menu available throughout.

Before you board, you'll have access to Lufthansa's business class lounge at JFK — typically the Lufthansa Business Lounge or, depending on timing, the partner lounge. Hot food, showers, and a genuine departure experience that costs nothing beyond the taxes you've already paid.

The flight itself is roughly 7.5 hours. In a flat bed, that's a real night's sleep — or close enough. You arrive in Frankfurt rested, which matters whether you're connecting onward or starting your trip immediately.

For the ChooseFI community: this is the redemption that pays for the honeymoon, the milestone anniversary trip, or the family adventure you've been planning since you first discovered travel rewards. The $6,500 you didn't spend stays in your index funds.

Book at the 355-day mark for peak dates. Lufthansa releases a small number of partner award seats when the schedule opens. The travelers who get summer business class awards are almost always the ones who searched on day 355. Set a calendar alert. Be ready.

Use Citi ThankYou for time-sensitive bookings. Because Citi transfers to LifeMiles are instant, you can find award space and complete your booking in a single session. This matters when you're racing to secure a specific seat before someone else grabs it.

Watch for LifeMiles purchased miles promotions. If you're 10,000–15,000 miles short of a redemption, purchasing miles during a 100% bonus promotion can effectively cost you 0.5–0.7 cents per mile — still leaving you with excellent overall CPP on the redemption. Check lifemiles.com regularly or set a Google Alert for "LifeMiles promotion."

Verify your booking on the operating carrier's website. After booking through LifeMiles, always look up your PNR on lufthansa.com or swiss.com. If it appears correctly, your seat is real. If it doesn't, call LifeMiles immediately.

Search Swiss as an alternative to Lufthansa. Swiss International Air Lines is also a Star Alliance member, also bookable through LifeMiles at the same rates, and also operates excellent business class products on transatlantic routes. Swiss often has more partner award availability than Lufthansa on certain dates.

Never transfer points before confirming availability. This is the most common — and most expensive — mistake in travel rewards. LifeMiles does not allow re-transfers out of the program.

Don't ignore the miles expiry policy. LifeMiles expire 12 months from your last earning activity. Any account activity — earning miles, purchasing miles, even a credit card transaction — resets the clock on your entire balance. Set a recurring reminder every 10 months to run a small transaction if you're holding a balance without an imminent booking.

Don't book Lufthansa First Class expecting the same experience. Lufthansa's First Class is a separate product with separate availability and pricing. Business class is the sweet spot here — outstanding value, more seats, and significantly better availability.

Avoid mixing Marriott Bonvoy transfers with tight timelines. At 3:1 and 1–5 days transfer time, Bonvoy is the slowest and least efficient path into LifeMiles. Plan accordingly.

This redemption is built for the experienced FI traveler who has already earned their first free night or domestic flight and is ready to graduate to aspirational international business class. You don't need to fly business class constantly — but for a honeymoon, a milestone birthday, or a long-planned European trip, this is the redemption that makes the experience extraordinary without derailing your savings rate.

You need 63,000 LifeMiles per person, per direction. A couple flying one-way needs 126,000 miles. Most people will get there through a combination of one or two travel credit card welcome bonuses and organic spending — entirely achievable within the FI community's existing card strategy.

## Rated: Hard — Here's Why This redemption earns its **hard** rating from three compounding challenges, none of which is insurmountable but all of which require deliberate effort. **Challenge 1: Award Availability Is Limited.** Lufthansa releases partner award seats conservatively. You may search for weeks before finding space on your preferred route and dates. This isn't a redemption where you decide Tuesday you want to go and book Thursday. You need a booking window of weeks to months, and flexibility in your travel dates helps enormously. **Challenge 2: The Booking Process Has Friction.** LifeMiles' website doesn't always surface partner award availability accurately. You'll likely need to search on United.com to find the space, then call LifeMiles to actually book it. That phone call requires patience — hold times vary, and you'll need to clearly communicate flight numbers, dates, and cabin class to the agent. **Challenge 3: Timing the Transfer Is High-Stakes.** Because you can't transfer points *out* of LifeMiles, you're committing your points the moment you initiate a transfer. Finding the seat, moving the miles, and completing the booking requires confidence and speed. ## How to Overcome the Challenges 1. **Start searching early** — 300+ days out for peak travel. Use ExpertFlyer alerts to monitor availability passively. 2. **Use Citi ThankYou for instant transfers** so you can act immediately when you find a seat. 3. **Practice the phone booking process** once before your high-stakes redemption — call LifeMiles with a simple question to understand their system. If you've successfully booked one or two simpler awards before, you can absolutely do this. The ChooseFI community has members completing this redemption every year — and every single one says the boarding moment made the effort completely worth it.

United shows Star Alliance partner availability that LifeMiles can book. Search for Saver-level awards in business or first class on your target route. Note the exact carrier, flight number, and date.

Log into LifeMiles.com and run the same search. Confirm the flight appears and note the exact mileage required. Some partner space visible on United may not be available through LifeMiles, so always double-check.

Amex MR, Chase UR, Citi ThankYou, and Capital One miles all transfer to LifeMiles. Amex transfers are often fastest (same day). Transfer only after confirming availability in Step 2.

Premium cabin award space is limited and competitive. Once your transferred miles appear in your LifeMiles account, complete the booking immediately. Do not wait — the seat can be taken by another searcher.

LifeMiles charges low taxes and fees on most partner awards, typically $30-$100 depending on the route. Some carriers (notably British Airways) add fuel surcharges, but most Star Alliance carriers keep fees minimal through LifeMiles.

Yes, Lufthansa First Class is bookable through LifeMiles at 87,000 miles one-way from the US to Europe. Availability is limited and typically released at 14 days before departure for partner programs, though some space appears further out. Search on United.com first to confirm Lufthansa First saver space exists before transferring.

Transfer times vary by partner. Amex Membership Rewards transfers often post same-day. Chase Ultimate Rewards transfers typically take 1-2 business days. Citi ThankYou and Capital One transfers can take 1-3 days. Always account for transfer time when booking time-sensitive award space.

Most Star Alliance carriers booked through LifeMiles have low or no fuel surcharges. The notable exception is British Airways (a oneworld carrier, not bookable through LifeMiles). Taxes and fees on typical Star Alliance routes run $30-$100 per ticket, making LifeMiles one of the cheapest programs for out-of-pocket award costs.

If the booking fails because award space disappeared between your search and transfer, the miles sit in your LifeMiles account. They do not expire as long as you have account activity every 12 months. You can use them for a future booking, but you cannot transfer them back to your original program.

Avianca LifeMiles is the program that lets FI-community travelers fly the same premium cabins as frequent business travelers at a fraction of the points cost. By consistently underpricing Star Alliance awards, LifeMiles turns 63,000-87,000 transferable points into experiences worth $8,000 or more. The process requires discipline (never transfer before confirming availability), patience (searching for saver space takes time), and a willingness to navigate a less polished booking experience. But the value is real: 15-20 cents per point on aspirational flights that would be unreachable at cash prices.

Liverpool could certainly benefit from his energy, versatility and ability to win possession. However, it remains to be seen whether they are prepared to meet Sunderland’s valuation when the transfer window opens in January.

Dakota Johnson and Machine Gun Kelly’s Full Relationship Timeline

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Dakota Johnson and Machine Gun Kelly have yet to define their relationship publicly, but the two stars have been spending plenty of time together.

The actress and musician were first linked in September 2026, when they were photographed together in New York City. Days later, a source told People that Johnson and Kelly, whose real name is Colson Baker, “have been hanging out and seeing each other under the radar for months now.”

The two stars had known each other before, but according to the source, they became closer after attending Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce’s wedding in July.

Since then, Johnson has watched Kelly walk the runway in Milan, and sources have shared more about how their connection developed.

Below, everything we know about Johnson and Kelly’s rumored relationship so far.

Johnson and Kelly both attended Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce’s July 3 wedding at Madison Square Garden in New York City.

The two stars already knew each other, but a source close to Kelly told People in September that they “hit it off” at the wedding.

On September 28, a source gave Entertainment Tonight similar intel, telling the outlet that Johnson and Kelly “really connected at Taylor and Travis’s wedding.”

On September 12, Johnson and Kelly were photographed together in New York City, getting out of the same SUV and entering Swift’s Tribeca apartment.

Four days later, a source close to Kelly told People that Kelly and Johnson “have been hanging out and seeing each other under the radar for months now.”

The source explained that Johnson and Kelly “started off as friends and were texting often, but now they’re spending more time together.”

“It’s a very new start, but they’re smitten with each other right now,” the insider added. “They haven’t put a label on their relationship yet. They are just seeing where spending time together will take them.”

A second source characterized things as “super casual” at that point.

On September 22, Johnson and Kelly both traveled to Italy for Milan Fashion Week and attended the same event.

Three days later, Johnson addressed the broader attention her dating life receives during an appearance on Vogue’s The Run-Through podcast, though she never mentioned Kelly specifically.

“Even if I go to dinner with my manager or my lawyer, it’s like, ‘Seen with mystery man,’” she said. “I also don’t care.”

When asked whether she ever feels compelled to correct speculation about her personal life, Johnson explained why she prefers not to engage with it.

“I think it’s just futile. People are gonna believe what they wanna believe, and I don’t have the energy to fight for that,” she said. “I would much rather focus on other things in my life. Every day, there’s something on the internet that’s ridiculous and true or not true, and I don’t care.”

On September 28, a source shared more about Kelly and Johnson’s relationship with Entertainment Tonight: “Dakota was the one who made the first move. He’s very shy around her, but he likes her a lot. It’s still new, but they’re having fun and seeing where things go.”

The insider added, “Dakota has been a fan of his music and has always found him cute. He likes her a lot, too.”

On October 19, a source close to Kelly told People that he and Johnson were spending more time together. “They have been out on dates that people don’t know about because there aren’t any photos.”

The insider said that they’re in the “dating phase,” and “aren’t ready to go official yet.”

“They both really like each other and have been seeing each other more frequently,” the source shared. “They haven’t put a label on their relationship yet, but they’re growing closer every time they hang out.”

In the past few months, they’ve especially connected over their “same dry and dark sense of humor,” the source said. “[They] make each other laugh.”

Days later, Kelly and Johnson were photographed out in Los Angeles on October 23. They first left a music studio together, riding off in Kelly’s red Maserati. That evening, they were seen together again, leaving the Chateau Marmont.

A source close to Johnson gave People an update on their dynamic on October 28. “She’s happy and enjoys spending time with him,” the insider said. “There’s a lot of chemistry and she’s been going to the studio with him while he works. For now, it makes her happy.”

On November 3, Kelly and Johnson were photographed leaving a restaurant in Calabasas, California, after a dinner date. They were smiling and talking to each other in the shots.

A Seagram Heir, a Bankrupt Hedge Funder, and More: Consignors for the November Marquee Sales Revealed

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

By Daniel Cassady, Harrison Jacobs, Alex Greenberger, Brian Boucher November 4, 2026 3:56pm

What a difference a year makes. The marquee New York sales return in just under two weeks, and while galleries still seem to be on a market seesaw, the auction houses have found a new, lucrative normal. When in doubt, just sell A+ material by established blue-chip artists with impeccable provenance. Sounds simple, right?

One suspects the auction houses’ specialists and rainmakers have found the task easier after a solid season last November, topped by Sotheby’s record-shattering sale of Gustav Klimt‘s Portrait of Elisabeth Lederer (1914–16) for $236.4 million. May was even more impressive: sales topped $2.5 billion across Christie’s, Sotheby’s, and Phillips, roughly double the $1.3 billion the three houses took in during May 2025. Related Articles Guillaume Cerutti to Lead the Château de Versailles After Short Stint at Pinault Collection Christie's First Fine Art Auction in Abu Dhabi Totals $8.94 M.

Provenance always matters in the art market, but in a more selective environment it has become essential to the success of each house’s sales. So much so that we seem to have moved from a time when each house had one or two single-owner mega-sales each season—like say, the $1.5 billion Paul Allen sale in 2022—to one in which a hefty share of the lots in each sale carry a named consignor.

To wit, 40 of the 79 lots in Christie’s 20th Century Evening Sale have named consignors, and that doesn’t include the seven lots carrying the Heroes & Lovers label, which it is an open secret were consigned by artist Jeff Koons. In Christie’s 21st Century Evening Sale, 35 of the 58 lots have a named consignor. Over at Sotheby’s, 22 of the 53 lots in The Now and Contemporary Evening Auction have a named consignor. The trend is less pronounced in the Modern Evening Auction, where only 13 of the 50 lots have a named consignor. But perhaps that’s because another 11 lots come from a single private American collection billed as “Shaping Modernity.” More on that one in a minute.

So why are there so many big-name collections coming to auction? Part of it is just timing, according to art adviser Meredith Darrow. Several major estates have come to market over the past few auction seasons, and auction houses have always been happy to put a famous collector’s name on a sale. But there’s another reason to make more noise about provenance these days. With the auction market still trying to recover from its post-Covid slump, a well-known name can help drum up interest and, potentially, push prices higher.

“Auction houses will do anything they can to promote the value of the works,” Darrow told ARTnews. “Calling out a notable provenance would and should increase interest and value of the work.”

It would be reasonable to assume, then, with all the named collectors, that there is little left for your favorite art sleuths to dig up in the paper trail of catalogues raisonnés, exhibition histories, and old press clippings. That would be wrong. There was no shortage of provenance breadcrumbs among this season’s lots, and ARTnews is happy to share them.

First off, as we reported last week, the cover lot of Christie’s 20th Century Evening Sale, Wassily Kandinsky’s Betonte Ecken (1922–23), was consigned by the Nahmads, the prominent art-dealing and collecting dynasty. It carries an estimate of $60–$80 million.

The same sale includes an untitled 1968 acrylic on paper by Mark Rothko (estimate $10–$15 million), which appears to have been consigned by Ed Rabin, the former president of Hyatt Hotels, and his wife, Jackie. The work, two crimson fields hovering over a scarlet ground, last sold in 1997, and a 2023 catalogue lists it as being in the Rabins’ collection.

Two lots in the sale carry the label “Surrealist Masterpieces from an Important American Collection,” and both point to Clarissa and Edgar Bronfman Jr. Edgar is a billionaire business executive and an heir to the Seagram fortune. The first, Wifredo Lam’s 1943 work Ogue Orisa (Eggue Orissa, l’herbe des dieux), estimated at $10–$15 million, appeared in MoMA’s recent Lam retrospective, where the Bronfmans were listed as lenders. The second, Roberto Matta’s Science, conscience et patience du vitreur (1944), carries an estimate of $5–$7 million. It also appears in a 2016 Architectural Digest feature on the couple’s penthouse.

The pickings were slimmer in Christie’s 21st Century Evening Sale. That’s partly because the works sold more recently, and partly because, as noted above, 60 percent of the lots already have a listed consignor. Still, we found that Ed Ruscha’s Male and Female (1987), estimated at $4–$6 million, was consigned by architect and real estate developer William Ehrlich and Ruth Lloyds. According to the provenance, they bought it directly from the artist, and Robert Dean and Erin Wright’s 2007 catalogue raisonné lists it in their collection.

Meanwhile, Vija Celmins’s Night Sky #21 (2009–13), estimated at $3–$5 million, appears to have been consigned by the Roman family, at least according to the catalogue for the Fondation Beyeler’s Celmins show last year. That appears to be Emannuel Roman, the CEO of investment firm PIMCO.

Back to Sotheby’s: in addition to the 11 lots in the Modern Evening Auction, one lot in The Now and Contemporary Evening Auction carries the “Shaping Modernity” label. A source with knowledge of the consignment told ARTnews that the consignor behind the group is George Weiss, the hedge fund founder now in bankruptcy. As Bloomberg reported in June, Weiss asked a Florida bankruptcy judge for permission to sell a 1972 self-portrait by Francis Bacon. Last month, Bloomberg reported that Weiss had struck a deal with Sotheby’s to sell more than a dozen works to repay a Bank of America loan. The Bacon will now be offered in The Now and Contemporary Evening Auction with an estimate of $10–$15 million. Additional Highlights include Paul Signac’s Collioure. Les Balancelles (Opus 167) (estimate $12–$18 million), Camille Pissarro’s Hameau aux environs de Pontoise (estimate $2.5–$3.5 million), Henri Matisse’s La Jetée à Collioure (estimate $5–$7 million), and Paul Cézanne’s L’Oncle Dominique (estimate $3–$5 million).

Two Agnes Martin works in The Now and Contemporary Evening Auction, Innocence (1999) and Untitled #2 (1991), were last exhibited at the Froehlich Foundation (Stiftung Froehlich). That would be the private museum outside Stuttgart, Germany, that was built to house the collection of ARTnews Top 200 Collectors Anna and Josef Froehlich. Martin’s catalogue raisonné lists the foundation as the owner of Innocence, and both works carry the label “Property from a Distinguished European Collection.” Connect the dots.

Then there’s Salman Toor’s Lavender Boy (estimate $60,000–$80,000). In a 2021 interview with Larry’s List, Rob and Eric Thomas-Suwall, the collectors behind the Instagram account @theicygays, named the Toor as the most treasured piece in their collection.

If you’re wondering which “Mid-Atlantic Institution” is deaccessioning its untitled Lee Bontecou sculpture, you need only look at the exhibition history. The work, estimated at $1.2–$1.8 million, has had just one owner and has been on view at Bryn Athyn College in Pennsylvania for the past 13 years.

Speaking of deaccessioning: Keith Haring’s portrait of Grace Jones (estimate $3.5–$5 million) was last exhibited at Museum MACAN in Jakarta, Indonesia, from 2021 to 2022. The museum’s 2021 exhibition guide lists the work as part of its collection. But as the Art Newspaper noted when the private museum opened in 2017, most of its collection comes from the holdings of its founder, Top 200 Collector Haryanto Adikoesoemo. So who the actual consignor is remains a little fuzzy.

Lastly, four works in Sotheby’s Modern Evening Auction and one in The Now and Contemporary Evening Auction carry the label “Magnum Opus: A Private Collection of Exceptional Art and Objects Through the Ages.” Which collector, pray tell, is behind such a weighty title? Edgar Degas’s 1882 drawing Danseuse rajustant son chausson proved to be the key to this particular treasure chest. According to Michel Schulman’s digital catalogue raisonné of Degas’s work, the drawing belongs to the Frederick Iseman Art Trust. The trust is also credited with the work in the catalogue for MoMA’s 2016 exhibition “Edgar Degas: A Strange New Beauty.”

The collection points to private equity investor Frederick Iseman and includes works by Georges Seurat, Pablo Picasso, Max Ernst, and David Smith in this month’s evening sales. Iseman, who founded CI Capital Partners in 1993, is also Helen Frankenthaler’s nephew and a former president of her foundation. He made headlines in 2023 when he sued his fellow board members, accusing them of exploiting his aunt’s legacy for their own benefit. The dispute turned particularly nasty, with accusations flying in both directions, before a New York judge dismissed the case on procedural grounds in September 2024 without ruling on the allegations themselves.

For all the attention Sotheby’s has lavished on the “Magnum Opus” collection, Iseman’s name appears nowhere in its marketing. And there has been plenty of attention: the collection numbers more than 900 objects, ranging from ancient Roman sculpture and old master paintings to Impressionist art and 20th-century design. More than 600 of them were offered across four dedicated sales in October, with a combined presale estimate of more than $60 million. Sotheby’s even took over four floors of the Breuer building for a public exhibition ahead of the auctions.

(And, as usual, spokespeople for Christie’s and Sotheby’s told ARTnews that they cannot comment on the identities of consignors.)

Hey, Siri: Plug into my app like you said you'd do way back in 2024

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

More than two years after promising it, Apple lets developers test Siri AI app integration

As of Tuesday, developers can explore expanded Siri AI integration for their Apple platform apps through beta releases of iOS 27.3, iPadOS 27.3, macOS 27.3, and visionOS 27.3. Next spring, nearly three years after announcing plans for deeper third-party Siri integration, Apple expects to begin accepting App Store submissions using the new capabilities.

"This developer beta offers a sneak peek at Siri AI app intents for developers," Apple said in a developer news post. "With this early Developer Preview, your app can work directly with Siri AI, giving you a first look at new ways to reach and serve your customers."

Anything is likely to be an improvement. Apple's Siri, a voice-activated assistant service, dates back to 2011 and gained limited third-party app access in 2016. In 2024, it became an interface for interacting with Apple Intelligence, its suite of generative AI features.

Apple Intelligence has not been very intelligent – several advertised Siri capabilities were delayed, so much so that Apple was sued in 2025 over allegations of misleading marketing. After a shakeup of Apple's machine learning group in December 2025, Siri and Apple Intelligence received a makeover at Apple's Worldwide Developers Conference in June.

"Before this year, Siri was a timer with a voice," said Roberto Gutierrez, Apple platforms staff engineer at a media company and author of the e-book On-Device Intelligence, in an email to The Register. "Weather, alarms, calls. Anything past that was a coin toss.

"On the developer side, SiriKit covered a handful of domains and Shortcuts only worked if users memorized the phrase. Siri AI is the first version that understands context and can chain actions across apps. That's a real change."

There is a catch, however, Gutierrez observed. Siri AI is available in a limited number of languages and regions. Support for Traditional Chinese is expected by the end of 2026, and EU availability remains a matter of negotiation – Siri AI is unavailable on iOS, iPadOS, and watchOS in the bloc as Apple works through interoperability concerns under Europe's Digital Markets Act.

"Apple is working constructively with the EU in an attempt to find a path forward," the company said last month without committing to any concessions.

Gutierrez said that he expects opening Siri to third-party developers will be useful to some extent.

"Siri can only do what apps expose through App Intents, and SiriKit is on its way out," he said. "If your app's main actions aren't exposed, the new Siri can't see you. The bigger shift is letting Claude, Gemini and others plug in. Developers already choose between three options for every AI feature: Apple's system model, an open model through MLX, or a cloud API. Siri is heading the same way. It doesn't need to be the smartest model. It needs to be the best router. That's the right call."

Apple has made choosing between those three options more difficult by failing to publish benchmarks that show how its Foundation Models compare with open-weight alternatives.

The biz has published measurements of how its third-generation Foundation Models compare to their 2025 predecessors. But the company's commitment – to "[share] more details on our latest foundation models, including updated evaluations and benchmarks, in a technical report later this summer" – remains unfulfilled so far as we're aware.

Gutierrez ran his own benchmarks to compare Apple's iOS system model (FM system, ~3B) to several open-weight models that are suitable for use on-device.

"On an A17 Pro iPhone running iOS 26, I tested Apple's system model against Qwen3 1.7B and 4B, Llama 3.2 3B and Gemma 3 4B on a structured-output task," he said. "Speed: Apple's model ran at about 149 tok/s, roughly 3x the fastest open model (Qwen3 1.7B, about 46)."

Quality, he said, was a tie, with every model passing five typed checks. But memory, he said, was where Apple's models had an advantage.

"The system model's weights live outside your app's memory budget," he explained. "My app peaked at 12 MB during inference. An open 4B model costs 2.4-2.7 GB, and a 2.4 GB model plus your app plus a WebView is a crash report. So yes, it's competitive, and on iPhone it's the default I'd recommend. If you want your own model, Qwen3 1.7B is the one."

But Gutierrez urged Apple to publish proper competitive benchmarks. "Their numbers compare against their own previous models," he said. "Developers aren't choosing between Apple 2025 and Apple 2026. We're choosing between Apple, Qwen and a cloud API. I had to build my own harness to answer a question Apple could settle with one table."

The absence of competitive model metrics is not the only gap. Apple has also failed to accommodate web apps.

"Web apps are locked out," said Gutierrez. "Foundation Models is a Swift API (and now Python). No JavaScript, no WebKit. A PWA's only option is to ship its own model into the browser and pay the full memory bill inside a WebView."

And bringing your own model may pose performance problems. "On an A17 Pro iPhone, the 3-4B open models pushed the phone into 'fair' thermal state and dropped to 12-24 tok/s," he said. "In practice, your own model on iPhone means ~2B or smaller."

With Siri AI unavailable on iPhones, iPads, and Apple Watches in the EU (and its rollout in mainland China subject to regulatory requirements), developers may need a fallback for global apps to handle regional restrictions. Apple's Private Cloud Compute offers eligible developers access to Foundation Models, Gutierrez observed, though it requires membership in the App Store Small Business Program, fewer than two million first-time App Store downloads across their apps, and the necessary entitlement. ®
